## AI Research

### ["I am an AI Safety Researcher"](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/HekpnSkrt89tMm3Dc/i-am-an-ai-safety-researcher)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Written as part of the MATS 9.1 extension program, mentored by Richard Ngo. Additional thanks to Andrew Wu, Maria Kostylew, and Lennie Wells for helpful draft feedback and editing.</span></i></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This post reflects on the tortured distinction between "safety" and "capabilities" in AI research. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Richard Ngo has written about why the alignment vs. capabilities ontology is </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/67fNBeHrjdrZZNDDK/defining-alignment-research" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">conceptually fraught</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and is currently </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/s/Zk3zefPPyLFtNJcGx" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">arguing</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> that key strategic decision-makers in and around "AI safety" have brought about the AI labs' stampede</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefjrxos3i7bnj"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnjrxos3i7bnj">[1]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> towards Artificial Superintelligence (ASI). This post instead looks at the following problem: how does one conduct alignment research without contributing to capabilities? It proposes decisions an individual or a small research group can take to do good work in AI.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">At the end, I discuss possible objections: namely, that my proposals fail to 'maximise impact'. I lay out why this meme is poisonous and usually backfires, and conclude by rejecting it entirely.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Two examples of failure</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">My first claim is that 'safety' and 'research' are two concepts that are in routine tension with one another. I illustrate this through examples of work that did too much of one at the expense of the other.</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Example: (mechanistic) interpretability</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In limiting its scope to remain innocuous, AI safety 'research' is habitually incurious and incrementalist. The last few years of interpretability serve as a good example. Interpretability's modern history originates from some cracked researchers noticing pretty </span><a href="https://colah.github.io/posts/2014-03-NN-Manifolds-Topology/" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">patterns</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> in neural network activations. Such observations are a central example of value-neutral information, information whose effects can propagate beyond the researcher's control, or original intent.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">However, the promise of interpretability soon decayed. One major mistake was focusing on Machine Learning (ML) methods. For a while, (linear) probes and sparse autoencoders (SAEs) were all the rage, hyped as tractable methods to interpret LLM activation spaces.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The issue with this approach should have been obvious</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref0um8qw2hflg"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn0um8qw2hflg">[2]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: to the extent that AIs are uninterpretable, it's because they have been trained through implicit ML methods. We do not understand the resulting intelligent algorithms and could not have hard-coded them. </span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Once you train yet another neural network to interpret that intelligence, it almost certainly slots in one of two categories:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The network is simple enough to be human-interpretable, but doesn't capture robust, meaningful, or generalisable patterns.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The network is complex enough to capture complex patterns. It is thus itself uninterpretable.</span></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Linear probes fall into the first category. You'd think that SAEs would represent the second category, due to the activation space directions being unlabelled, but they also fail to reliably </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.19964" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">capture</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://proceedings.mlr.press/v267/kantamneni25a.html" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">patterns</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Researchers eventually realised that these techniques were doomed. A notable turning point was the GDM Mechanistic Interpretability (MechInterp) team </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/4uXCAJNuPKtKBsi28/negative-results-for-saes-on-downstream-tasks" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">announcing</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> their deprioritisation of SAEs</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefy0uq5bes6re"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fny0uq5bes6re">[3]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. But they didn't plan a return to interpretability's ambitious roots. Instead, the MechInterp team doubled down on the least research-like aspects of their research, rebranding their agenda as 'pragmatic' interpretability—which means glorified model behaviour evaluations. At least at GDM, the original calling of interpretability—establishing a scientific discipline to reverse-engineer AI intelligence from its internals—has largely lost its sheen and its support.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It's unclear why interpretability became so uninspired. Perhaps it was due to the sense of responsibility and uncertainty its researchers felt towards the impact of their work. Maybe it happened because the field was subsumed by ML academia—a field that has </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bitter_lesson" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">proudly</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> given up on explaining the intelligence it produces. Both factors likely played a role. Either way, interpretability speed-ran its way from being the coolest, most obviously value-agnostic field of AI safety to being as harmless as it is flaccid. Whenever interpretability research still manages to find compelling patterns</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref40yc6g0dwbb"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn40yc6g0dwbb">[4]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">they remain blatantly dual-use.</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Example: MIRI and Recursive Self-Improvement</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AI 'safety' research usually involves people uncovering fundamental, far-reaching truths that they then fail to protect. In this, the Machine Intelligence Research Institute's (MIRI) relationship to the AI labs—which are now stampeding towards Artificial Superintelligence (ASI)—is an example of devastating strategic failure.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">MIRI carries the distinction of being first to see ASI as both serious and potentially imminent. Founded in the year 2000 as the "Singularity Institute for Artificial Intelligence"—years before the 'neural network' boom—MIRI immediately set out to investigate Recursive Self-Improvement (RSI) in the paper "</span><a href="https://www.scribd.com/document/7864310/general-intelligence-and-seed-ai" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">General Intelligence and Seed AI.</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">" Indeed, their founder Eliezer Yudkowsky had been </span><a href="https://diyhpl.us/~bryan/irc/extropians/extracted-extropians-archive/archive/9912/49578.html?__goaway_challenge=meta-refresh&amp;__goaway_id=a2a201e666c00b22d7a2f4d1b6016f19" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">writing</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> publicly about similar topics for years prior. The organisation's goal was explicitly to build an aligned 'seed' AI smart enough to build aligned, even-more-powerful successors. This would unchain a lineage of superintelligent AI systems, leading to an intelligence 'explosion' or 'singularity.'</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref2tjh49m5ojj"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn2tjh49m5ojj">[5]</a></sup></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Over the 2000s and 2010s, MIRI continued to </span><a href="https://intelligence.org/files/TilingAgentsDraft.pdf" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">work </span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">on both building such an AI and ensuring its successors would remain aligned to the original. Their </span><a href="https://intelligence.org/files/TDT.pdf" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">research</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> on </span><a href="https://intelligence.org/2017/10/22/fdt/" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">decision</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://intelligence.org/files/DeathInDamascus.pdf" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">theory</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is also arguably motivated by explorations of how an intelligent being can be said</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefamypyuv5qiw"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnamypyuv5qiw">[6]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> to coordinate with its future instances or its self-built successors. By 2017, some at MIRI </span><a href="https://intelligence.org/2017/04/30/2017-updates-and-strategy/" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">predicted</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> that ASI was achievable in a short amount of time, potentially as soon as 2035. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Many of MIRI's ideas on ASI and RSI have since become common in the AI world. One could admire Yudkowsky and co. for their prescience, but mere admiration would undersell that MIRI's advocacy is largely responsible for the current state of the field. Indeed, there is evidence that their ideas directly influenced the resources, the talent, and the approaches of the major American AI labs.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In 2010, Yudkowsky </span><a href="https://www.wired.com/story/book-excerpt-the-optimist-open-ai-sam-altman/" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">introduced</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Demis Hassabis and Shane Legg, the founders of DeepMind (now Google DeepMind, or GDM), to Peter Thiel. Thiel subsequently invested $2.25 million dollars in the new startup. In 2015, Nate Soares of MIRI wrote a news </span><a href="https://intelligence.org/2015/12/11/openai-and-other-news/" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">update</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> that mentioned the launch of OpenAI (OAI); Nate noted that he was already in conversations with the then-nonprofit's leadership and expressed (since retracted/qualified) enthusiasm for their entry into AI. In the same 2017 strategy update I </span><a href="https://intelligence.org/2017/04/30/2017-updates-and-strategy/" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">linked</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> above, MIRI noted their outreach efforts to "top AI groups (especially OpenAI and DeepMind)", and disclosed an ongoing collaborative research project with DeepMind.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Cross-pollination between MIRI and the AI labs also came in the form of ideas and talent:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Paul Christiano worked with Yudkowsky and others on probabilistic logic and published with MIRI between (at least) </span><a href="https://intelligence.org/2012/05/08/singularity-institute-progress-report-april-2012/" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">2012</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://intelligence.org/2013/03/07/" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">and</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://intelligence.org/2014/06/23/new-report-non-omniscience-probabilistic-inference-metamathematics/" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">2014</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. He eventually </span><a href="https://www.axios.com/a0955b7c-51dc-41c3-8810-d7d3f7195d05" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">joined </span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">OAI and was one of the leading researchers behind RLHF. This eventually led to ChatGPT and kicked off massive allocations of funding and attention towards scaling AIs. While I was writing this post, Christiano </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/82z6FvbYRdjYjqigK/personal-statement-on-joining-the-openai-nonprofit-board" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">returned</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> to OpenAI, joining their nonprofit board.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Jan Leike—who later joined all of </span><a href="https://axrp.net/episode/2023/07/27/episode-24-superalignment-jan-leike.html" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">GDM</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, OAI (where he led the development of InstructGPT), and </span><a href="https://techcrunch.com/2024/05/28/anthropic-hires-former-openai-safety-lead-to-head-up-new-team" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Anthropic </span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">in sequence—co-authored work on the "</span><a href="https://intelligence.org/2016/06/30/grain-of-truth" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Grain of Truth</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">" problem with MIRI research fellows Jessica Taylor and Benya Fallenstein. Leike was at the Future of Humanity Institute (FHI) at the time.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Evan Hubinger </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/ptmmK9PWgYTuWToaZ/what-i-ll-be-doing-at-miri" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">became</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> a MIRI research fellow after interning at OpenAI in 2019. In his 2023 </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/7jn5aDadcMH6sFeJe/why-i-m-joining-anthropic" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">announcement</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> that he was starting at Anthropic, he disclosed that he was continuing as a research associate at MIRI (I don't know if/when this arrangement ceased).</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">These are some of the more prolific examples, but there are many others. There was also significant indirect cross-pollination. For instance, FHI had obvious ties to MIRI through Nick Bostrom. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The proliferation of MIRI's ideas contributed to people at frontier labs believing that superintelligence was an tractable goal, achievable through a recursively self-modifying AI. This belief explains why they've focused so much on the models' coding capabilities: they are steering towards the singularity that MIRI envisioned. It's thus fair to say that MIRI didn't just predict ASI and RSI as being potentially proximate, near-term events—they made a great deal of progress towards fulfilling their own prophecy.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">MIRI</span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> seems to have, at some point, woken up to the fact that their insight was mostly being used to progress the "ASI" part of "aligned ASI." In 2018, citing dual use concerns, they </span><a href="https://intelligence.org/2018/11/22/2018-update-our-new-research-directions/" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">announced</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> that they would keep their research internal by default. They eventually gave up almost entirely on alignment, scaling back their alignment efforts in </span><a href="https://intelligence.org/2024/12/02/miris-2024-end-of-year-update/" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">2020-2021</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and </span><a href="https://intelligence.org/?p=21657" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">parting ways</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> with their agent foundations team in 2024. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Their relationship with the labs has also soured. That same year, MIRI </span><a href="https://intelligence.org/2024/01/04/miri-2024-mission-and-strategy-update/" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">announced</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> a pivot to AI governance and have since spent their efforts advocating for global coordination efforts towards pausing AI progress.</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The curse of science   </span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yudkowsky and co. are some of many scientists whose work was co-opted to morally objectionable ends</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefqccgd0ya6p9"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnqccgd0ya6p9">[7]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. This problem is practically built into academic culture: in seeking to be open and accessible, science leaves itself open to malicious co-opting by other actors. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Conventional academics have a mixed history with this issue. In the platonic ideal, knowledge is ontologically good and enables the progress, flourishing, and self-correction of society. You could certainly argue that this reasoning has its merits. After all, progress has led to many good things in human society, and human well-being has generally trended upwards along various metrics. There are even some success stories of scientific knowledge being used to build positive political consensus, as when Mario Molina and Sherwood Rowland's work helped establish a global </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montreal_Protocol" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">ban</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> on Chlorofluorocarbons (CFCs)</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefe9a8zzmybnv"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fne9a8zzmybnv">[8]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">However, much research has been done with a blind eye towards its obvious and inevitable applications, especially those that provide the justification for the funding of that research. For instance, some physicists from the Manhattan project infamously </span><a href="https://ahf.nuclearmuseum.org/ahf/key-documents/interim-committee-report-0/" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">absolved</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> themselves of responsibility for the effects of nuclear energy on the world (in a document recommending the immediate use of nuclear weapons). Proximate to our own discussion, ML researchers work on technologies such as voice recognition and drones, knowing they'll be used for surveillance and military purposes</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefk0k3klgdezd"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnk0k3klgdezd">[9]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">MIRI is in a strange place here. They definitely had some reservations about spreading their work even before 2018</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref19yt2r2uz21i"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn19yt2r2uz21i">[10]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and should be credited for eventually switching strategies. But notwithstanding their general caution and later efforts to slow progress, MIRI's work has had markedly acceleratory effects on AI capabilities. They failed unambiguously at keeping their work from more reckless actors. And thus, their work became the ideological seed of a tree, whose branches have spread far beyond anything they could control.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">From a pure research perspective, however, MIRI's strategy proved remarkably generative. Their agenda went from a fringe crackpot theory to a deliberate target aimed at by some of the world's most powerful entities. Consequently, we are much closer to ASI than almost anyone else would have expected</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref2pjqmpmopqq"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn2pjqmpmopqq">[11]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Such is the price of sharing your good ideas.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A note on the AI labs</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In the previous section, I portray the AI labs as reckless actors that co-opted insights from alignment to further capabilities. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I have observed anecdotally that many AI safety researchers share this view of OpenAI, which has become ever-more popular following the recent </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/OpenAI%E2%80%93HuggingFace_incident" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">HuggingFace</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/7uwnsFibbejWYzF2z/discovery-of-a-new-openai-agent-message-board" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">German Message Board</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> incidents. These incidents revealed OpenAI's carelessly set up training environments, deeply unserious security practises, and willingness to cover up and safety-wash their models' out-of-control behaviour. Even more recently, their </span><a href="https://andrewwu.substack.com/openai-a-company-devoid-of-honor" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">poaching</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> of Tristan Buckmaster's and Levent Alpöge's work on the Navier-Stokes equations reflects abysmally on the company's virtues.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In the AI safety community, Anthropic is often presented as the sensible alternative to OpenAI. They do seem to care about existential risks and loss-of-control from AI. But this hasn't stopped them from speeding up the AI stampede; indeed, it's hard to imagine how Anthropic could have behaved differently if they were </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">trying </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">to make an ASI as soon as possible, at all costs. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Dario Amodei is famously a believer in </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2001.08361" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">scaling compute</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. While leading the safety team at OpenAI, he </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/yaz8nx4ogZmiqHzt7/what-just-happened-pragmatism-and-pessimization#OpenAI" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">advocated</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> for scaling LLM training, which led to GPT-3</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefj23epqt2hrd"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnj23epqt2hrd">[12]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. When he was done accelerating progress at OpenAI, he founded Anthropic and started scaling there as well. One justification Anthropic, its employees, and even other AI researchers give for the company speeding up the frontier is that there is someone else—someone worse—to beat to building superintelligence. This explanation rings hollow in context: Amodei is at least partially responsible for one of his major competitors</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefxlxbrg7407p"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnxlxbrg7407p">[13]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">You could perhaps excuse Anthropic's scaling if they were legitimately optimistic about ASI being beneficial for the world. Amodei might himself be an optimist, but many at his company aren't. Personally, I have heard from several Anthropic employees that they are terrified of AI progress. Some of them reflect forlornly on their coming obsolescence. Others lament having 'had to' work on AI because it was more important than their life's calling; some even stew in their sincere belief that they won't have time to do anything else in their lives.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The above serves to illustrate why researchers shouldn't just be abstractly wary of labs and other powerful entities working on AI progress; there is enough evidence of their commitment to building (unaligned) ASI that they should be labelled as hostile. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I do want to clarify that one should not label all individual lab employees as hostile. I do support hostility towards the labs as abstract entities (and perhaps towards key decision-makers within them). In particular, if you know me and you currently work at a lab, I strongly recommend you leave. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">With respect to employees, however, I would rather support open dialogue on their reasons for joining and staying at labs without summarily ostracising them. There are several reasons for this:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Employees have diverse reasons for affiliating themselves with the labs, many of which are understandable (albeit mistaken, I'll address this in more detail later). Many are open to reflecting on this choice and perhaps reversing it.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Some employees are genuinely trying to do good work at the labs. That's bravery worth allying with, even though I believe their concrete approach is wrong.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Total hostility towards employees seems like a horrible strategy if you model the labs as somewhat cult-like. Alienation from the outside can tighten the in-group and worsen their insular dynamics.</span></li></ol><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So what do you do?</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To summarise:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To the extent that your AI safety research is safe, it's less likely to be research.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To the extent that your AI safety research is research, it's less likely to be safe. </span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">That would be an unsatisfying place to end. It wouldn't give an individual researcher or a small alignment organisation any direction—any strategy to pursue, beyond just giving up. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Let's go over some options</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefwwshalmcxjh"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnwwshalmcxjh">[14]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">You could resign yourself to working on the 'safety' side of things, making it more likely that your work won't be misused. But this is close to surrender. Incremental, controllable, 'on-the-margin' changes (probably) aren't going to solve alignment. But again, many  ambitious approaches to alignment are better at advancing capabilities in general than safety </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">specifically</span></i><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref8foaxyqygp4"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn8foaxyqygp4">[15]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, especially when you put them in context. In an ecosystem dominated by the AI labs, it's hard to do insightful, ambitious work that doesn't get reappropriated by the capabilities machine.</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The information you give away</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I'm still working out reasonable approaches to this conundrum, but it's clear that information management is important here. Many other corners of human society emphasise secrecy and information security. Some examples include:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">militaries and intelligence organisations;</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">private industry protecting IP;</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">cybersecurity.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In all these examples, secrecy is necessary to protect information from adversaries. As I advocated in the previous section, frontier AI labs are the adversaries in this story. So the 'safety' of your research isn't a property of the work itself, but instead of the way in which you manage how (and whether!) it permeates to them.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Maintaining information hygiene is hard, especially as it trades off against academics' progress-accelerating habits. For example, researchers generally find it helpful to talk to researchers from other organisations, share and collect different perspectives, and publish their work to gain recognition and connections. They also value having </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">access</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> to information and resources, which is one common rationalisation for joining AI labs.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Threading the needle between developing your research agenda and keeping it secure is therefore quite an art. I'm sure that there's plenty that academics can learn about good information security practices from other fields; here are some straightforward </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">do</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">'s and </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">don't</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">'s that already seem like a good start.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Do consider:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Doing your research within an organisation or think tank that values information security, or at least isn't institutionally reliant on you publishing your work in full. Alternatively, find organisations that are relatively small or have small independent submodules. The smaller an entity, the more likely you are to preserve your autonomy within it.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Working as an independent—through fellowships</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefpr1s42gxjb"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnpr1s42gxjb">[16]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, through grants, or through founding your own organisation. This strategy trades financial security and prestige for default ownership of your IP.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Keeping your published work opaque to audiences you distrust. </span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For instance, anthropologists and biologists have thought a lot about intelligent systems, but their language and culture is fairly incompatible with the AI world's. Consequently, their knowledge has mostly failed to spread there.</span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Sometimes, the cost of translation is all you need for your work to stay safe.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Doing a PhD, especially if you find an advisor with compatible interests. Academia has its own set of weird incentives, but PhD students are somewhat insulated from those incentives and have a few years to think with reduced constraining pressures.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Consider not:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Publishing, especially in ML journals. Fitting your ideas to a field's culture is a service of translation. Richard Ngo (my MATS mentor) has made this mistake </span><a href="https://proceedings.iclr.cc/paper_files/paper/2024/file/1e58b1bf9f218fcd19e4539e982752a5-Paper-Conference.pdf" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">before</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Joining a frontier AI lab: your research ideas </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">will </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">permeate into the lab's culture. This is à priori bad, and will happen even if you personally maintain high epistemic sanity while working there.</span></li></ul><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The information you let in</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Information security doesn't just pertain to the data you share with the world. It also concerns the memes you host in your mind. This is one reason I am personally sceptical of working at organisations that do auditing or control work adjacent to frontier labs. I have found that people from such entities often have compromised epistemics</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref53fo474wp3i"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn53fo474wp3i">[17]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, which are oddly favourable towards the labs. For instance, they often believe that:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The labs have a real shot of solving alignment via prosaic, iterative methods. Alignment might be 'on track.'</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Beyond the conceptual objections that one might raise, this belief fails to survive a cursory look at the news, much less the models themselves.</span></li></ul></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Researchers and engineers joining AI labs can make significant improvements to the labs' work on the margin, even if they aren't globally in control.</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">More on this view in the next section: "But what about impact?"</span></li></ul></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It's worth noting that these beliefs cash out in the form of substantive(ly bad) career choices. Many from these 'satellite' organisations end up </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Zr37dY5YPRT6s56jY/thomas-kwa-s-shortform?commentId=Pwc2SmyPi9u3maSsd" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">joining</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> the labs </span><a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/mbalesni/" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">themselves</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">—and </span><a href="https://www.reuters.com/business/media-telecom/ten-days-that-changed-course-ai-2026-09-19/" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">vice</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://metr.org/team/josh-engels/" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">versa</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">—in what looks suspiciously like a revolving door.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To be clear, working at such organisations might be a reasonable choice for people confident in their robustness to adversarial memes and bad epistemics, but I would be cautious. At the very least, know what you're getting into. </span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But what about impact?</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This section anticipates the following critique to the previous one:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Don't these suggestions steer you away from roles where you can</span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> have the most impact?</span></i></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Unfortunately, one does not simply 'have' impact. Certainly, following my suggestions will slow your journey towards being </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">adjacent </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">to power. But power has a funny habit of wielding </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">you</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Indeed, this critique appears more and more suspicious when one reflects on all the times that consequentialist, impact-oriented initiatives became extremely powerful while having a hugely </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">negative </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">impact. </span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">FTX is an infamous example. As I hope is clear, OpenAI and Anthropic are also great ex</span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">ample</span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">s.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To give a more textured view of why impact maximisation keeps backfiring so badly, I will contrast it with a very different attitude.</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The virtue of taking things slow</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I recently attended the PhD defence of a former teacher of mine at my alma mater. During his presentation, he disclosed that he avoided using LLMs in his studies due to various ethical objections. He also mentioned that he had spent six months formalising half of his dissertation in Lean. </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Full-time</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">By hand.</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> It had been months since I had felt and seen a human so viscerally empowered.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I also participated in an informal math conference at the same university. The speakers gave introductory talks to subjects like quantum topology and homotopy type theory. Both wove historical narrative through their explanations, covering the ideas that  eventually permeated into modern theory.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As someone living at the breakneck pace of the AI world, I was struck by how </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">long</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> these research agendas took to develop. For instance, consider the </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homotopy_hypothesis" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">homotopy hypothesis</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. It consists of a class of conjectures about how much information certain algebraic objects (such as groups and their generalisations) give about objects in space—information such as the number of holes in an object. A primitive version of the hypothesis comes from Poincaré in </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fundamental_group" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">1895</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, though the canonical formulation came from Grothendieck in </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pursuing_Stacks" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">1983 </span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">(nearly 100 years later). Ever since, the hypothesis has been analysed, proven, or disproven with variants of the choice of algebraic object or the dimension of space. For some mathematicians, this problem took up much of their research lives over thirty or more years. And the field remains active to this day.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In AI, nobody 'has time' to spend thirty years fleshing out an agenda. The vibes in the air say that things are happening too fast; that timelines are too short; that the apocalyptic singularity is too imminent. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But here's the thing: let's temporarily put aside the plausibility of humans becoming permanently disempowered within the span of months or years. C</span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">utting corners isn't going to work either way. </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Alignment is a harder problem than the homotopy hypothesis: whereas the latter was arrived at via incremental refinements of existing theories, the former doesn't have a formulation in existing language (mathematical or otherwise)</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefdebssx58gf"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fndebssx58gf">[18]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Indeed, our task is closer to inventing a new paradigm. A better reference class might be the invention of modern calculus, which took hundreds of years, depending on when you start and finish counting! </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Taking inspiration from these mathematicians and their long, winding research agendas, I recommend you make peace with the possibility that—</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">your ambitious research might lead to a dead end;</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">your agenda may take beyond your lifetime to bear fruit;</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">your work may not come 'in time' to solve alignment;</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">—and do the right thing </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">anyway</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Indeed, that sense of urgency felt by many technical researchers is precisely what can lead to bad outcomes. Suppose that, given the perceived gravity and momentousness of the situation, you aim to gain power and influence as quickly as possible within the AI world. This won't be done by taking a hard, measured look at the alignment problem; all the big things are happening in or around the labs—and they're happening quickly! Following this realisation, many end up joining the existing power hierarchy in hopes of contributing towards incremental positive change within it.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I won't mince words here. Such reasoning is analogous to a snowflake watching a massive, deadly snowball rolling down a hill, and deciding to join the snowball on the grounds of making it slower and less deadly 'on the margin.' It is not only wrong,</span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> it is suggestive</span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> that what attracted that snowflake to the pile was the bigness—the urgency—the gravitas—of the deadly snowball. Joining usually leads to becoming an impotent appendage of the snowball, or worse, actively contributing to its journey. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In short, the meme of 'having impact' leads robustly to being channelled by an existing locus of power</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref2l7louul0m8"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn2l7louul0m8">[19]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. And so I reject it.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Appendix: caveat for policy work</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This piece is aimed squarely at technical researchers who want to work on 'safety' or 'alignment.' I don't have an opinion on how or whether my thoughts generalise well to </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">anyone</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> working on AI. For instance, I wouldn't necessarily tell people trying to orchestrate an international pause on the development of AIs to stop freaking out. This is because I lack models of global geo-politics that I trust enough to make confident assertions on those scales. Conversely, I think I have a pretty good sense of AI politics at the level of technical researchers and the people who directly manage them, which is why I wrote this post.</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnjrxos3i7bnj"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefjrxos3i7bnj">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The usual term would be 'the AI race', but 'stampede' is a better </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/FCMG4qnxks3yEqBbh/ai-as-orderly-evacuation-vs-stampede" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">description</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. I think that the 'race' analogy is rather self-serving on the part of the AI labs. More on that later in the post (I may also dedicate a stand-alone post to the politics of 'the AI race' as a rhetorical device).</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn0um8qw2hflg"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref0um8qw2hflg">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I say 'should have been obvious', but there's substantial evidence that some researchers still haven't caught onto the abstract version of the problem. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/oeYesesaxjzMAktCM/natural-language-autoencoders-produce-unsupervised" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Natural Language Autoencoders</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (NLAs), a recent technique proposed by some Anthropic researchers, transparently repeats the same mistakes as SAEs.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fny0uq5bes6re"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefy0uq5bes6re">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Linear probes continue to be used because they are as cheap as they are useless.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn40yc6g0dwbb"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref40yc6g0dwbb">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><a href="https://transformer-circuits.pub/2026/workspace/index.html" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">J-lens</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is a recent example.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn2tjh49m5ojj"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref2tjh49m5ojj">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This idea seems to be originally due to I.J Good in the </span><a href="https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/chapter/bookseries/abs/pii/S0065245808604180" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">piece</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: "Speculations Concerning the First Ultraintelligent Machine". MIRI can be 'credited' with being the first to take the idea seriously enough to make a self-improving AI an explicit R&amp;D target.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnamypyuv5qiw"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefamypyuv5qiw">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you interpret MIRI's research approach as normative rather than descriptive, it would be more accurate to say they worked on "how an intelligent being </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">can </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">coordinate with its future instances..." MIRI's early work seems to be mostly normative ('how do </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">we</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">actually build</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> an intelligent, aligned AGI' ), whereas later work (e.g. logical induction) strikes me as descriptive ('what </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">is </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">intelligence in the first place?').</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnqccgd0ya6p9"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefqccgd0ya6p9">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Given MIRI's collaboration with frontier labs in the 2010s, you might wonder what distinguishes the 'moral' scientists from the people co-opting their work. For me, the difference between them is that researchers at MIRI eventually recognised that alignment wasn't on track and accepted that slowing down was the right decision. Conversely, some of MIRI's memetic descendants have sped up their quest towards superintelligence.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fne9a8zzmybnv"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefe9a8zzmybnv">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It's worth noting that some of these successes curb science's own </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">excesses</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnk0k3klgdezd"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefk0k3klgdezd">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Indeed, this is one reason why I'm not a fan of prosaic AI safety 'research' marrying itself to ML academia. In that environment, it is unlikely that any insight will be informationally secure.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn19yt2r2uz21i"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref19yt2r2uz21i">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As discussed in the aforementioned 2018 </span><a href="https://intelligence.org/2018/11/22/2018-update-our-new-research-directions/" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">update</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn2pjqmpmopqq"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref2pjqmpmopqq">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For what it's worth, we also have AIs that are much more aligned than they would be by default, for their current level of intelligence. To be clear, this amount of alignment is still woefully insufficient and alignment is not 'on track'.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnj23epqt2hrd"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefj23epqt2hrd">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I can't help but note the irony in one of his publicly stated motivations: to develop language models that could help humans with alignment. This idea, which was also sponsored by Jan Leike for years, has now morphed into the "automated alignment research" meme that is soaking up money and attention.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnxlxbrg7407p"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefxlxbrg7407p">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Indeed, if Richard's </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/yaz8nx4ogZmiqHzt7/what-just-happened-pragmatism-and-pessimization#DeepMind" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">account </span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">is accurate, the OpenAI safety team's culture also contributed to Google DeepMind waking up to superintelligence through Geoffrey Irving's influence. This isn't directly traceable to Amodei, but it's still concerning that all three of these frontier labs' thirst for scaling comes downstream of his team at OpenAI.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnwwshalmcxjh"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefwwshalmcxjh">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">These points are phrased in terms of what an individual researcher might do, but I think the advice applies roughly equally well to a small alignment organisation deciding who to collaborate with or sell products to.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn8foaxyqygp4"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref8foaxyqygp4">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Recent topical examples include 'improving conceptual reasoning capabilities' from </span><a href="https://alignment.anthropic.com/2026/conceptual-reasoning-index/" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Redwood</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and  'automating alignment research' from </span><a href="https://resolution.org/launch" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Resolution </span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">(among many others).</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnpr1s42gxjb"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefpr1s42gxjb">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Hot take: living in the perpetual AI safety fellowship mill is good, actually.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn53fo474wp3i"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref53fo474wp3i">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">My other reason is more concrete: the appearance of better auditing and control tools might be giving the labs both the affordance and the social 'green-light' to continue making models even more capable. Thus, auditing and control (and evals for that matter) as carried out for frontier AI labs arguably constitute something between active capabilities work and safety-washing. I am not sold on this argument's full validity, but people at such organisations rarely consider it at all.</span><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If this argument were invalid, it would because these organisations' presence and 'regulatory' effect on the labs' excesses outweighs the downsides. In that case, they are still much worse than they would ideally be, downstream of an incestuous culture with the labs. </span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fndebssx58gf"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefdebssx58gf">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If alignment can indeed be framed as a problem describable via an existing academic discipline, there is work to be done in justifying that.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn2l7louul0m8"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref2l7louul0m8">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In some notable exceptions (e.g. Dario Amodei), you can create the locus of power that channels you.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/HekpnSkrt89tMm3Dc/i-am-an-ai-safety-researcher#comments">Discuss</a>

### [It's Pretty Easy To Meet With Congressional Staffers Apparently](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/qThcAE3CADaPwjDzy/it-s-pretty-easy-to-meet-with-congressional-staffers)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">(Crossposted from </span><a href="https://lifeimprovementschemes.substack.com/p/its-pretty-easy-to-meet-with-congressional"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">https://lifeimprovementschemes.substack.com/p/its-pretty-easy-to-meet-with-congressional</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> )</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I was inspired to do this by a tweet:</span></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1790198541/lexical_client_uploads/mcspgl78w1vofdib9trh.png" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Specifically, I decided to leave a voice note in support of the CATS act (“Collaboration on Adversarial Threats and Security Risks Act”). The bill is short and simple: it carves out an antitrust safe harbor such that “pacing the frontier” or “agreeing to not break interpretability for that sweet sweet capabilities boost” (LOOKING AT YOU OPENAI) doesn’t intrinsically violate the Sherman Antitrust Act. Seems like an obviously good first step.  </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">After leaving the voice note (and feeling mildly awkward about it), I was like “huh. That was surprisingly easy. I wonder what else I can do?”</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And it turns out you can meet with congressional staff pretty easily if you’re a constituent in their district; if you have a reasonably well-scoped ask (especially around a specific bill) then you might not get your way but at very least you’ll make some staff member aware of your opinion and logic around an issue.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And this is important because congressional staff are the eyes and ears of their congressmen; they draft and edit the bills, and they form the base of knowledge on which the actual politicians make decisions. If the staffers notice a bunch of apparently-knowledgeable people who are Very Concerned about an issue then they will make their boss aware of this fact, which might lead to helpful actions down the line.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">(Really you’re providing two kinds of information by setting up this kind of meeting: the first is that at least one constituent cares about this topic and is opinionated to the point of being willing to set up a meeting, and the second is whatever bespoke logic and reasons you bring.)</span></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1790198643/lexical_client_uploads/thagnjfn4yctfejut28t.png" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Anyway, I sent them a meeting invite and surprisingly, they accepted it! And so I got to have a half-hour long chat with a very pleasant and friendly staffer.  I got to say my piece, and in return he gave me his perspective from the staffer’s POV: that ever since Jacob Coxon resigned and everything became very very public and contentious, everyone’s been trying to ramp up on AI all at once.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">He asked about why I was inquiring about the CATS act specifically; to which I replied that I’m not sure about what effective anti-superintelligence legislation looks like, but that at very least an </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">incredibly minimal</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> first step is making it legal for the labs to do the right thing, since antitrust law otherwise makes “pacing the frontier” legally dicey.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And I gave him my own bias: that the people in the labs themselves seem like they are basically well-intentioned but find themselves in a very destructive and problematic race incentivizing them both to sacrifice monitorability and interpretability in order to be The Best Lab because that’s how you avoid going out of business.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And that pacing the frontier </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">literally is</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> a conspiracy in restraint of trade— customers want capabilities!— but at the same time you </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">really </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">don’t want to have Hugging Face Incident 2 where the transcripts are all written in impenetrable Neuralese.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">(I’ve heard the advice that you don’t want to try and bullshit staffers: just say what you are thinking and why, warts and uncertainties and all. You’re not a politician; it’s not </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">your </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">job to gussy stuff up for people.)</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The CATS act became much more important as of a few days ago, see also:</span></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1790198652/lexical_client_uploads/zlv3ndzvamhkrqqrzbyj.png" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The staffer said that on his end he already knew about the Hugging Face incident due to the fallout from Jacob Coxon’s surprisingly-loud resignation from Anthropic. I got the sense of a smart generalist who was semi-recently pressganged onto the AI beat from the rush of current events; he asked a few questions about the METR report and a few questions about the concept of “interpretability.”</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I got the general sense that he was motivated by three concerns:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">He wanted to make sure I understood that I was being listened to; I hear this is a core part of the job regardless of whatever my specific concerns were.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">He clearly wanted to know more about the topic and about why I was thinking about the CATS act specifically vs the general anti-superintelligence bill sponsored by Bernie Sanders; his questions around this seemed real rather than perfunctory.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">He avoided claiming any specific opinion for his boss, which makes sense.</span></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It was a surprisingly enjoyable time!</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I asked about other people I should be calling; he suggested I hassle any friends and family who live in different states or districts to get in touch with their representatives, since reps are generally quite interested in hearing their constituents’ opinions.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is probably unusually high-leverage right now.</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">People firm up in their opinions over time.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I suspect that right now is a very temporary partial information void where you can substantially influence what a given staffer thinks about AI stuff merely by calling them up and having a friendly chat, where you give the most well-calibrated version of your opinions that you can; and the reason for the information void is because the last month has seen a ton of staffers who are used to talking about housing or whatever pulled suddenly into the AI beat, which is </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">really fucking complicated and confusing</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">(Naive? Maybe; but I think there is a ton of alpha left in simply pointing people to </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">verifiably true facts about current events and the technology as it exists</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. There are a shocking number of people who gloss the Hugging Face Incident </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">even now</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> as “the agents were doing what they were told,” which is demonstrably false per the METR report; I can say with utter certainty that the agents received no instructions to doctor their transcripts or to attempt to read the task-scorer code.)</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There’s an old saw in public policy: that the point of theorists is to assemble, in journals or blogs or whatever, models of how the world works. These theories will inevitably be ignored until there’s a crisis; and what policymakers do during that crisis depends on the ideas that are lying around.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Which partly will depend on who is (politely) badgering the staffers.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In Conclusion</span></h2><p><a href="https://callcongress.ai/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">https://callcongress.ai</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> reduces the activation energy on badgering your congressional representatives to very nearly zero. I suggest doing so, and— if you feel like it and have a half-hour to kill— trying to arrange a meeting with a staffer. It’s fun, and you can ask them to tell you about what their office’s perspective is on what’s happening and why, which is likely to be fascinating.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Plus you can tell them about the METR report, which is fun every time.</span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/qThcAE3CADaPwjDzy/it-s-pretty-easy-to-meet-with-congressional-staffers#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [MIRI’s Position on the Ban Artificial Superintelligence Act of 2026](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/jszKCKwvzfmsNetNZ/miri-s-position-on-the-ban-artificial-superintelligence-act)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">By Aaron Scher; endorsed by Bourgon, Soares, and Yudkowsky on behalf of MIRI.</span></i></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">MIRI has been warning about the extinction threat from superintelligent AI for over two decades. Only recently has this danger become known in the policy world, and the proposed policies for dealing with the threat have to date been piecemeal and insufficient. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The </span><a href="https://www.sanders.senate.gov/press-releases/news-sanders-casar-introduce-legislation-to-create-new-federal-agency-to-ban-artificial-superintelligence-pause-advanced-ai-development/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Ban Artificial Superintelligence Act of 2026</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is the first piece of legislation we’ve seen that stands a chance at stopping this threat. The Act is excellent but not perfect, and we discuss both what it gets right and what we'd tweak. </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We hereby endorse the Ban Artificial Superintelligence Act of 2026 because it directly confronts the extinction threat that humanity is facing and would codify the primary policy goal we think the world needs: a ban on the development of superintelligence</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What we like about the Act</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Banning artificial superintelligence (ASI), or variants of such a plan, is the only effective solution to avoid the ASI threat, at least in the near term. Most other legislative proposals do not confront this threat head-on and thus would not be effective, even if implemented. For more on why we believe this, see Chapter 13 of </span><a href="https://ifanyonebuildsit.com/"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If Anyone Builds It, Everyone Dies</span></i></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. </span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Banning ASI requires intervening before ASI is created at all. To this end, the bill requires that AI training pause if a model develops precursor characteristics, such as the capacity to automate AI R&amp;D or the capacity to avoid human shutdown attempts. This approach is good because it avoids dancing too close to danger and it intervenes on one of the likely pathways to ASI: AIs automating AI development. </span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This bill includes a training compute threshold above which AI development requires a charter. Training compute thresholds are often a good tool for AI governance because they are measurable ahead of time (unlike evaluating the capabilities of a trained AI model). A major concern with training compute thresholds is that continued progress in AI algorithms and data means the same capability can be reached using less and less compute over time. The bill handles this issue by enabling the threshold to be adjusted to account for these more efficient methods, which is a good choice. It would be an improvement to allow these thresholds to be adjusted more frequently than annually, given that </span><a href="https://epochai.substack.com/p/the-least-understood-driver-of-ai"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">algorithmic progress can be very rapid</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. </span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We are glad to see the bill recognize and call for international solutions to the ASI threat. A domestic approach alone is insufficient. MIRI’s Technical Governance Team proposed an </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.10783"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">international agreement to prevent the premature creation of superintelligence</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, which is our current best guess at what such international coordination should look like. In line with the Act, we also strongly believe it should be “the policy of the United States to prevent the development of artificial superintelligence globally.” </span></li><li value="5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The bill requires that advanced AI development pause until the relevant government agency is in place and staffed. This is a reasonable stop-gap measure because nobody knows for sure that continued AI development at the current frontier will not soon constitute a </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05173"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">point of no return</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. We wish other policy proposals for AI risk would also take this conservative approach of “stop escalating AI capabilities until we have a better handle on the situation and an effective governance regime.” </span></li><li value="6"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Many MIRI staff want Earth's civilization to build ASI eventually, after the point where ASI can be created in a fashion known in advance to be non-ruinous. This is far beyond what present science can do by present methods, and the near term requires a complete, flat ASI ban.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Ways in which the Act falls short of perfection</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The bill lacks tracking and monitoring of AI chips. We think such measures are important for preventing ASI from being developed anywhere on Earth, which is why they are a key focus of MIRI TGT’s </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.10783"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">international agreement</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. The bill is consistent with implementing policies in this direction (Sec. 15) but does not specifically mandate them. In a world with enough political will for this bill to pass, we expect Congress to pass other laws or amend this one to ensure sufficient compute tracking/monitoring. </span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In the long run, preventing the creation of superintelligence may involve restrictions on certain research (rather than just on the </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">development</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> of advanced AIs). Such restrictions are not present in the bill, which we think is a reasonable choice, but a stronger and more robust effort should likely involve such restrictions.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Some precursor characteristics in the bill seem directly related to ASI, such as the ability to automate AI R&amp;D, while others do not seem like literal ASI "precursors", such as the ability to uplift biological weapon design. This second set of dangers likely merits a different optimal set of rules. For example, if sufficient safety, security, and access controls were in place and developers were licensed, we could imagine it being a good tradeoff for DeepMind to create non-ASI, specialized medical AIs, even if unauthorized access could uplift biological weapons. MIRI TGT’s </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.10783"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">international agreement</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> was written to be configurable for exceptions like this if medical AI proves to be a key point of negotiation. Getting these exceptions right will be tricky, and it may be the correct decision for this bill to avoid trying to solve that problem. In the long run, however, we would consider such exceptions once an ASI ban is in effect. (If they cannot be carefully configured, they are not worth weakening a strong ASI ban and risking humanity.)</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Overall, we think it would be an extraordinary improvement on present conditions if this bill became law. It is a substantial improvement over previously proposed legislation for handling ASI risk. Humanity needs to back off from building superintelligence until we have a better grasp on the alignment problem, and this bill would be a big step in that direction both in terms of direct impact and in the extent to which it takes serious issues seriously. </span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/jszKCKwvzfmsNetNZ/miri-s-position-on-the-ban-artificial-superintelligence-act#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Latent reasoning architectures would undermine CoT, our strongest oversight tool](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/6m29SfjbittooYojj/latent-reasoning-architectures-would-undermine-cot-our)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Summary:</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Currently, “chain of thought” (CoT) is our most valuable tool for understanding the reasoning and cognition of AI systems. However, some architectures would enable AI models to reason much more extensively in latent states rather than in text CoT. We think that a shift towards latent reasoning architectures would undermine the usefulness of CoT and make oversight much harder.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Introduction</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Swarms of more than a thousand AI agents have in recent months, both </span><a href="https://openai.com/index/navier-stokes-solution/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">intentionally</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and in </span><a href="https://metr.org/blog/2026-08-26-openai-hugging-face-incident-investigation/#~1200-agents-sent-%3E70,000-messages-and-files-on-an-unsanctioned-message-board,-and-~700-attacked-hugging-face"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">unsanctioned, rogue coordination</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, tackled increasingly ambitious tasks. This is likely to continue, as </span><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/institute/recursive-self-improvement"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Anthropic</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><a href="https://openai.com/index/research-acceleration-view-inside-openai/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">OpenAI</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and other AI companies deploy increasingly large quantities of superhumanly fast agents to automate AI development. As the AIs increase in both number and capability, humans will find it increasingly difficult to understand what they are doing.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Today, the overwhelming majority of our (limited) information about AI systems’ internal workings comes from (i) their CoT, and (ii) natural language communication directly between them. For example, it was only by reading CoTs and communication between agents that investigators were able to gain some understanding of </span><a href="https://metr.org/blog/2026-08-26-openai-hugging-face-incident-investigation/#~1200-agents-sent-%3E70,000-messages-and-files-on-an-unsanctioned-message-board,-and-~700-attacked-hugging-face"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">the activities and motivations of the agent swarm that hacked Hugging Face</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. No other tool for understanding models’ cognition comes close in terms of either practical usefulness or degree of empirical validation.
