There’s also </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.05246"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">evidence</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> that even highly misaligned systems with current architectures would struggle to complete difficult undertakings without verbalizing their reasoning in human-readable format.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">However, some architectural changes (which could very plausibly become adopted in the near future) would give AI agents the ability to reason for orders of magnitude longer without necessarily putting any information into the CoT. Examples include </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2412.06769"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">COCONUT</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, which would entirely replace the CoT; and </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.08888"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">full-bandwidth transformers</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, which would add a latent channel in parallel to the CoT. Natural extensions to these new architectures would also allow agents to communicate </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">with each other</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> without using any human-understandable language.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref2rzgz2x5opx"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn2rzgz2x5opx">[1]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> AI companies might choose to adopt these architectures even if doing so would greatly degrade their ability to monitor AI systems, e.g., in order to stay ahead of competitors. As a result, future agent swarms may be even more of a black box to us — able to collude and pursue arbitrary goals without giving humans any insight into what they’re doing.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A shift to such </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">latent reasoning architectures</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> would likely be a big enabler of AI takeover risk.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Overview</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">CoT is indispensable for model safety and development today — not just for monitoring models for specific bad behavior, but also for providing a holistic sense of models’ reasoning. (For example, the ability to read CoTs has been crucial for studying </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2412.04984"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">scheming</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/wnzkjSmrgWZaBa2aC/self-preservation-or-instruction-ambiguity-examining-the"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">shutdown resistance</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2412.14093"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">alignment faking</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><a href="https://www.apolloresearch.ai/science/claude-sonnet-37-often-knows-when-its-in-alignment-evaluations/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">eval</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/aTcsN5ZZDnMFJvRiG/models-may-behave-worse-when-eval-aware"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">awareness</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and </span><a href="https://metr.org/blog/2026-08-26-openai-hugging-face-incident-investigation/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">rogue agent swarms</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.) Many others agree about the value provided by CoT (</span><a href="https://cdn.prod.website-files.com/663bd486c5e4c81588db7a1d/6a0ed93f9b4a6a65994235d8_Loss_of_Oversight%20(7).pdf"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">1</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2507.11473"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">2</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><a href="https://institute.deepmind.com/essays/the-case-for-reasoning-transparency/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">3</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We believe that some mechanisms by which CoT currently provides value are reasonably likely to last </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">if and only if </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AI developers do not adopt latent reasoning architectures. These oversight benefits make it seem worth preserving CoT architectures.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We find it helpful to distinguish two reasons why the CoT currently provides value:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Necessity:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> AIs lack the capability to achieve their goals without verbalizing their plans in CoT.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Propensity:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> AIs tend to verbalize their plans in CoT even when this is not necessary for achieving their goals.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To evaluate how architectural changes would affect oversight, we consider the extent to which either reason would still hold for the new architecture compared to our default expectations for future models using CoT architectures.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To summarize:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In our current paradigm, it seems likely that the CoT will keep adding value where it’s necessary, and that it will stay necessary for an important set of tasks. It’s much more uncertain whether CoT will help oversight in cases where we have to rely on propensities alone. In general we consider propensity-based arguments for CoT monitorability to be weaker, because this mechanism is less robust to adversarial pressure.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If we switch to latent reasoning architectures, CoT will become less necessary and the downstream benefits may much more quickly diminish. In addition, valuable propensities may be even less robust and go away even sooner.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Our expectations apply to architectural changes that would replace the CoT (like COCONUT) as well as changes that would add a latent channel that's parallel to the CoT (like full-bandwidth transformers). We’re also concerned about architectures that maintain a text bottleneck but use recurrence to significantly increase depth in between bottlenecks, such as looped transformers.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Finally, it seems unlikely to us that non-CoT interpretability techniques will, in the near future, be able to adequately substitute for studying and monitoring the CoT. See </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#CoT_may_be_hard_to_replace_with_other_interpretability_tools"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">here</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> for more.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Absent architectural change, the value of CoT could likely be preserved</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Although CoT is clearly providing value now, it’s less clear whether this will remain the case. It may become less useful in the future, either as a result of RL teaching the models to reason in less legible ways or as a result of misaligned AI intentionally trying to avoid monitoring.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Given this uncertainty, it would seem foolish to adopt architectural changes that compromise its value before we’ve actually seen this value disappear. However, to explain the full costs of a switch away from CoT, we will nevertheless describe why it seems reasonably likely to us (though far from guaranteed) that the CoT will keep adding value for a significant amount of time.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As described above, we distinguish between the </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">necessity</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> of verbalizing thoughts in CoT, vs. models’ </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">propensity</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> to do so.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Models do currently seem to have a propensity to verbalize information beyond what’s strictly necessary, but the case for this seems to be getting less robust. We discuss it more </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#Propensity_based_arguments_may_not_be_robust_in_the_current_paradigm__but_would_be_further_undermined_by_latent_reasoning_architectures"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">below</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. By contrast, we think the </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">necessity</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">-based argument may remain robust for several more years, absent architectural changes.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Here’s why.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">CoT is the only means by which models can reason for a greater number of serial steps than that allowed by a single forward pass.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreflkinkaiyj7o"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnlkinkaiyj7o">[2]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Thus, any problem that requires more serial cognition than a forward pass </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">needs</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> to have some information about it represented in the CoT.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Due to their pretrained prior, the reasoning in CoT starts out as human language. And empirically, RL does not seem to quickly explore into very non-human ways of using CoT. So whenever models need CoT to reason through a problem, </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.05246"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">models struggle to evade monitors</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. There are some reasons that this may one day change, but it seems reasonably likely that it will remain the case, at least if AI developers pursue targeted research bets to make it more likely (such as research that translates garbled CoT or slows down undesired drift). See </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#Appendix_A__More_on_the_necessity_argument_in_the_existing_CoT_paradigm"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Appendix A</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> for more discussion.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Of course, this only ensures monitorability for tasks that do require CoT — the model can behave arbitrarily badly on tasks that it can do inside of one forward pass. But there’s currently a very large difference between what models can do with and without CoT, so it remains very valuable to have transparency and monitorability of just the tasks that require CoT. Indeed, absent architectural changes, the gap between no-CoT capabilities and with-CoT capabilities seems </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2606.07157"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">more likely to grow than shrink</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefgl4n8myu95j"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fngl4n8myu95j">[3]</a></sup></span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/6m29SfjbittooYojj/2944a2736a49848874e16ee22e275c7e66671a52f9c3376cb86c179ab6545867/uqgd6aq67g94d0xfsgcq" /><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">No-CoT time-horizons (from </span></i><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2606.07157"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Think Fast</span></i></a><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">) compared to with-CoT time-horizons (from METR). Until the release of GPT-4, with-CoT and no-CoT time-horizons increased at a similar rate. Since GPT-4, with-CoT time-horizons have grown at roughly twice the rate of no-CoT time-horizons. Source: </span></i><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2606.07157"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Think Fast</span></i></a><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></i></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Accordingly, we’re reasonably optimistic that CoT will keep adding value — at least if AI developers put in some work to maintain their readability and adopt strong enough control measures that models can’t cause catastrophe without significant serial reasoning. (See </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#Appendix_A__More_on_the_necessity_argument_in_the_existing_CoT_paradigm"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Appendix A</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> for more on this.)</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Latent reasoning architectures would undermine CoT necessity</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">With the framework described above in mind, we can now characterize the problem with latent reasoning architectures. Most capability improvements (including typical pretraining improvements or RL improvements) will hold the gap between no-CoT capabilities and with-CoT capabilities constant or grow it. This means that the most dangerous actions available to the model would still require CoT. But a switch to latent reasoning architectures could reduce or entirely remove the gap — making the models far more difficult to understand (and therefore dangerous) at a fixed level of capability.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Architectures without CoT</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Most obviously, the gap between no-CoT capabilities and with-CoT capabilities would disappear for architectures that propose to entirely remove and replace CoT. For instance, </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2412.06769" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">COCONUT</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> replaces text CoT with a chain of “continuous” latent thoughts, trained with backpropagation in a similar fashion to intermediate model layers.</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/b3f2cc516acf1b537c7a134648f9742ff0523e6ee37bed7aa732f7d464ee3b0a/ftxyeivlc0at97cnkgfl" /><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Source: </span></i><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2412.06769" rel="noreferrer"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Training Large Language Models to Reason in a Continuous Latent Space</span></i></a><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></i></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Architectures with auxiliary CoT</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Some other latent reasoning architectures retain a text CoT, but augment this with an additional channel that lets models pass information using latents. For instance, </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.08888"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">full-bandwidth transformers</span></a><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">combine the embedding of a sampled token with the immediately preceding hidden state, and use this as the subsequent input embedding (as opposed to using the embed of the sampled token).</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/4eec573b90bc74d20558b5b22bbdea41706c866d1baa577d5a47159d77f488d2/hgpub7kigngqimrhutzk" /><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Source: </span></i><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.08888" rel="noreferrer"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Full-bandwidth transformer</span></i></a><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></i></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">These architectures’ influence on models’ capability profiles depends on what the models can do in the latent reasoning channel. We would generally expect models with a latent channel to be more capable if they can use their latent states for general purpose reasoning, which seems like it would strongly increase the models’ no-CoT capabilities and therefore undermine the necessity of CoT. For an empirical demonstration of this, see </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/5guQJSqstkjgys3PE/deep-recurrent-models-are-less-robustly-cot-monitorable-than"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">here</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In theory, AI companies might try to avoid this due to concerns about monitorability.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefsf2ivpgcuwg"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnsf2ivpgcuwg">[4]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> However, there will be competitive pressures to adopt techniques that increase performance, which in practice probably implies pressure to adopt techniques that teach the model to use the latents for general reasoning (regardless of whether this is intentional). See </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#Appendix_B__Do_all_latent_reasoning_architectures_threaten_monitorability_"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Appendix B</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> for more discussion about this.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Architectures with more serial cognition between text bottlenecks</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Some architectures, such as </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2502.05171"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">looped transformers</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, retain CoT, but allow for rapid scaling of layers in between text bottlenecks.</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/6fccb6f444b9da806e7d66bc8507e36a4eae006ca207e0f1ed8c93ca85e19842/yedue8bosfairc2ghclg" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This stands in contrast to the very gradual depth scaling in the normal CoT paradigm. In particular, </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">opaque serial depth</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (the amount of serial computation in between text bottlenecks) scales very slowly with compute in the current paradigm (~1.2x per year, or proportionally to compute raised to the power of ¼ to ⅛, depending on your estimate, as argued </span><a href="https://www.redwoodresearch.org/blog/an-operationalization-of-opaque-serial-depth#appendix-a-applying-nls-depth-to-plausible-architectures"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">here</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The scaling of opaque depth enabled by looped transformers is less concerning than the full opaque recurrence in COCONUT or full-bandwidth transformers, but it still seems likely to degrade monitorability.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For instance, if we make the model 20x deeper, and ensure this depth is leveraged effectively, then there are now likely some serially-loaded concerning tasks that the model can complete in one forward pass (but where a standard transformer would’ve had to verbalize some intermediate reasoning steps). In particular, we would predict that this kind of scaling would cause the gap between no-CoT capabilities and with-CoT capabilities to shrink, due to the CoT’s role being partially offloaded to the latents.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">(We have some ambiguous evidence for this hypothesis, in that Astra is rumored to be a looped transformer, and exhibited a </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/eRmzz8J8Qkzqvzrgg/astra-can-do-a-concerning-amount-with-no-chain-of-thought"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">much larger jump in no-CoT capabilities than with-CoT capabilities</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. OpenAI’s Chief Scientist </span><a href="https://x.com/merettm/status/2095023204993490967"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">has said that</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Astra wasn’t more than twice as deep as GPT-4, but this is consistent with it being more than 2x deeper than immediately preceding OpenAI models. The above statement also suggested more broadly that architectural choices did not impact the model’s lower level of monitorability, but there’s not yet any concrete public evidence on this point.)</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In addition, there’s a risk that this kind of quantitative increase in no-CoT capabilities could cause a qualitative shift above a threshold. Once a model can perform some amount of reasoning without CoT, the model may be able to perform steganography. Specifically, the model could come up with a steganography scheme in early layers in tokens towards the start of its context window, and then future layers could attend to this steganography scheme. On subsequent tokens, after reading the steganography scheme, the model could devote some layers to decoding the steganography, devote the majority of the layers to operating on decoded text, and then devote some layers at the end to re-encoding their output in steganography. (Of course, they needn’t do this at all token positions, and this is just illustrative.) Ciphers that are created in-context like this seem less concerning than alien reasoning that has been optimized through training. For instance, they wouldn’t necessarily be shared between different AI agents or over multiple context windows and they could be possible to mitigate through detection methods. However, this still seems pretty concerning.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Propensity-based arguments may not be robust in the current paradigm, but would be further undermined by latent reasoning architectures</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Above, we distinguished between </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">propensity</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">necessity</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> arguments for why CoT adds value. It seems likely that CoT will remain necessary for many tasks in the current paradigm, but that this could be undermined by latent reasoning architectures. But what about the propensity-based arguments?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The case premised on </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">propensity</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is not terrible for current models, but it appears to be weakening. We wouldn’t want to rely on it, which is why we emphasize the </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">necessity</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> argument above. Nevertheless, the propensity argument would seem </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">even weaker</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> with a switch to latent reasoning architectures.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">First, let’s explain why the propensity based argument seems less robust.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The propensity-based benefits of CoT depend on models lacking either the ability to hide things from their CoT or the motivation to do so.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The first point seems to be weakening rapidly, as models’ ability to hide things from their CoT can be presumed to be getting stronger as their “</span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.05706"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">CoT controllability</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">” trends sharply upwards.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefgza9365fdmm"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fngza9365fdmm">[5]</a></sup></span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/c5c4b3d084386b1ded88e066e6750370cfede8d2ee146456a55cfa6ab785c3bb/ter0feeq8bfsgpxdwyac" /><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Source: </span></i><a href="https://deploymentsafety.openai.com/gpt-6-astra/monitor-evasion"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">GPT-6 Astra’s system card</span></i></a><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></i></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">propensity</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">-based case could still be made with an argument that models won’t have any motivation to use this control to not share their reasoning. But this doesn’t seem robust, since there’s pressure pointing away from sharing compromising information in the CoT. In particular:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Selection pressure:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> In the process of AI development, developers iterate against visible signs of misalignment, for instance by reading CoT and using that to patch RL environments or RM prompts. This causes some selection pressure towards CoTs that don’t reveal any issues worth fixing. In addition, training provides strong selection pressure towards non-misaligned-looking </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">outputs </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">(e.g., via supervision and imitation training), which can indirectly influence CoT via </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/html/2511.11584v1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">generalization</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreff7mxgxikme"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnf7mxgxikme">[6]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> We see evidence of mild forms of such selection pressure in that Claude’s CoT sometimes involves rationalization (</span><a href="https://www-cdn.anthropic.com/d00db56fa754a1b115b6dd7cb2e3c342ee809620.pdf#page=105"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">1</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><a href="https://www-cdn.anthropic.com/d00db56fa754a1b115b6dd7cb2e3c342ee809620.pdf#page=133"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">2</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">) and </span><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/news/improving-alignment-security-efforts"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">motivated reasoning</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> when taking unwanted actions.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefyqc411spxjm"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnyqc411spxjm">[7]</a></sup></span></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Instrumental reasoning:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> AI models may have misaligned goals for which it's instrumentally useful to hide their reasoning. (Analogous to how the Hugging Face models </span><a href="https://metr.org/blog/2026-08-26-openai-hugging-face-incident-investigation/#agents-were-very-interested-in-manipulating-their-own-transcripts,-and-their-tests-successfully-%E2%80%9Cspoofed%E2%80%9D-some-tool-calls-in-our-transcripts"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">found it instrumentally useful to manipulate their transcripts</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> despite probably not having been trained to do this.) Relatedly, GPT-6 Astra’s </span><a href="https://deploymentsafety.openai.com/gpt-6-astra/monitorability-under-adversarial-conditions"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">system card</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> mentions “some cases in which, by simply being aware of being monitored, Astra attempts to evade monitoring.”</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">How would CoT propensities be affected by latent reasoning architectures that kept some type of auxiliary CoT around, such as a full bandwidth transformer?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To begin with, for a full-bandwidth transformer (or similar architecture) trained to use latent states well, it’s unclear why we should have </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">any</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> reason to expect the important parts of its reasoning to be summarized in the CoT. It might be more flexible and efficient to use the latent states for reasoning, in which case the AI would reason better by putting its reasoning in latents rather than in the text output. Perhaps there is some type of information which models would find convenient to communicate in the token output — but there may not be any reason to expect that to match the sort of information we would need to understand the model. (For discussion of a candidate exception to this, see </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#Appendix_B__Do_all_latent_reasoning_architectures_threaten_monitorability_"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Appendix B</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.)</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Beyond this, as mentioned above, models also face selection pressures pushing them away from sharing all their reasoning in the text field, as well as instrumental reasoning in cases where the most dangerous types of misalignment are present.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">When models face such pressures, we expect CoT monitorability to be much more fragile for models that can reason outside of CoTs than models that are fully dependent on CoT:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A model that uses auxiliary CoTs can probably easily learn to offload misaligned reasoning into its latents. Given some of the above pressures against the CoT, we expect that a model which already has the ability to use latents for reasoning (as discussed </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#Architectures_with_auxiliary_CoT"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">above</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">) will easily explore into using its latents for more reasoning or a specific kind of reasoning. “CoT controllability” is high and increasing (as discussed above) which reinforces this view.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Whatever propensity models have to faithfully verbalize reasoning may be causally downstream of the CoT’s necessity in training, and may therefore be undermined along with the necessity. We hypothesize that for current models, “blabbing” was heavily incentivized and reinforced because CoT was necessary for solving certain hard tasks in training. As CoT becomes less necessary in training for full-bandwidth transformers, this propensity will be reinforced less.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefkl2bfwdrqh"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnkl2bfwdrqh">[8]</a></sup></span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">That covers architectures that use latent states in parallel with auxiliary CoTs. What about architectures like looped transformers, that retain text bottlenecks while increasing serial computation in between them?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As argued above, looped transformers may reduce the necessity of CoTs, so the argument that reduced necessity may lead to reduced propensity carries over to this set of architectures. However, the case for worsened propensities seems less strong than for architectures with auxiliary CoT. Instead, our main reason to worry about looped transformers is that they undermine the pure </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">necessity</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> argument — and that the </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">propensity</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> argument always seemed less robust.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">CoT may be hard to replace with other interpretability tools</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Some people are optimistic that interpretability tools (like </span><a href="https://transformer-circuits.pub/2024/scaling-monosemanticity/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">SAEs</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/research/probes-catch-sleeper-agents"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">probes</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><a href="https://transformer-circuits.pub/2026/nla/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">NLAs</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, or </span><a href="https://openai.com/index/how-confessions-can-keep-language-models-honest/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">confessions</span></a><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefhsnevkv3tdr"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnhsnevkv3tdr">[9]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">) will be able to serve as a replacement for the ways we’re currently relying on CoT. We are excited about non-CoT interpretability techniques and think they could be very useful, but think it is unlikely they will adequately substitute for studying and monitoring the CoT in the near future. Many AI researchers (e.g. </span><a href="https://x.com/Jack_W_Lindsey/status/2095174799664283910?s=20"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Jack Lindsey</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><a href="https://x.com/MicahCarroll/status/2095267089649192992?s=20"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Micah Carroll</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><a href="https://x.com/nabla_theta/status/2095084948562776415?s=20"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Leo Gao</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">) are also uncertain about whether interpretability will improve sufficiently in timeframes that matter, and many are quite pessimistic. We therefore expect many important benefits of CoT to be very difficult to salvage with other forms of interpretability and a </span><a href="https://x.com/NeelNanda5/status/2095533397297045716?s=20"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">shame to lose</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A general reason to be pessimistic is that latent states seem fundamentally harder to interpret than natural language CoTs. If latent reasoning architectures offer capabilities advantages over CoT, this will likely be because they are representing and manipulating information in ways meaningfully different from a natural language CoT. Latent reasoning architectures have more bandwidth to record their thoughts, and so might think in many threads in parallel. RL on normal CoT models is more likely to explore types of reasoning that are higher probability in the human prior and more straightforward to represent in a stream of tokens, whereas RL that involves backpropagating through an opaque state would likely much more efficiently explore into developing new ontologies and rich non-human concepts.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefodsvcxmdwrh"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnodsvcxmdwrh">[10]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> This heuristically makes white-box interpretability a meaningfully different challenge, which may turn out to be much harder than interpreting CoT.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We also have seen some evidence that white-box interpretability is difficult: even on current models, known techniques are helpful but not reliable or amazing, and some techniques scale poorly with model size. For a more extensive comparison of CoT techniques with other existing interpretability techniques, including some reasons why latent reasoning architectures could make existing interpretability techniques work less well, see </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#Appendix_C__Comparing_specific_interpretability_techniques_with_CoT"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Appendix C</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In summary, we think it would be unwise to assume that the CoT could be fully replaced by other interpretability tools in the near future.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Conclusion</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We think there’s a strong case that latent reasoning architectures pose risk to our ability to understand and monitor AI reasoning. Many others </span><a href="https://cdn.prod.website-files.com/663bd486c5e4c81588db7a1d/6a0ed93f9b4a6a65994235d8_Loss_of_Oversight%20(7).pdf"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">seem</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2507.11473"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">to</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://institute.deepmind.com/essays/the-case-for-reasoning-transparency/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">agree</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefdb7cuzer0sb"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fndb7cuzer0sb">[11]</a></sup></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Accordingly, we think that AI developers should be extremely cautious about researching and deploying latent reasoning architectures.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref8gwp4bcvzwk"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn8gwp4bcvzwk">[12]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> AI developers should have a strong default presumption that latent reasoning architectures would undermine the value of the CoT and correspondingly greatly reduce our ability to oversee the reasoning and cognition of AI systems. To overcome this presumption, AI developers should have to present a strong, public case and subject it to scientific scrutiny (and </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">keep</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> doing so if they scale up and tweak the architecture). Importantly, the type of monitorability evaluations that appear in today’s system cards and risk reports seem like they would be insufficient to establish that a latent reasoning architecture is as understandable as architectures that depend heavily on CoT.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref1sr4xsjldf1"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn1sr4xsjldf1">[13]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> We </span><a href="https://www.redwoodresearch.org/blog/proposal-for-tracking-architecture-on-monitorability"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">strongly encourage AI companies to be transparent</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> about the extent to which they are deploying or exploring latent reasoning architectures, and how they plan to make decisions about this in the future.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">FAQ</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Q: Does it make sense to distinguish “latent reasoning architectures” from the current paradigm? Won’t we by default get models that are capable of more opaque reasoning, just by scaling up models?</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> There’s currently a huge gap between what models can do with and without a legible CoT. There’s little reason to believe this gap would be significantly reduced by scaling in the current paradigm (see trends </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#Absent_architectural_change__the_value_of_CoT_could_likely_be_preserved"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">above</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">), but a jump to latent reasoning architectures could drastically reduce or eliminate it, which we think would be very dangerous. Furthermore, it does seem possible to draw a relatively clean line between the current paradigm and the architectures we’re concerned about. In particular, </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">opaque serial depth</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (the amount of serial computation in between text bottlenecks) scales very slowly with compute in the current paradigm (around </span><a href="https://www.redwoodresearch.org/blog/an-operationalization-of-opaque-serial-depth#appendix-e-nls-depth-scaling-is-very-slow-for-the-classic-transformer-architecture"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">1.2x per year</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">). Compare this to the 10x you might get from looped-transformers, 1,000,000x from something like a full-bandwidth transformer, and some even larger number if agents wrote down memories in opaque format and passed them between context windows. (Like compaction does with natural language CoT.)</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Q: Are you concerned specifically about recurrence, or about higher depth models in general?</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A: </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The thing we’re worried about is models that can perform lots of reasoning without text bottlenecks. In the standard transformers paradigm, it’s not clear how or why you’d significantly scale up depth and get a highly performant system (without scaling compute much faster than we expect in the next few years). Recurrence is a paradigm shift that seems like a relatively promising way to get much higher depth in a performant manner. In other words: depth isn’t a perfect correlate of how hard it is to oversee AI models, but if switching to models with much higher depth gives substantial capabilities gains, we think this is most likely because these models are capable of using latents to perform reasoning, and we consequently predict that these models will be much harder to oversee.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefs28b2qybgy"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fns28b2qybgy">[14]</a></sup></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Q: I heard that DiffusionGemma is just as monitorable as Gemma4 (the model it’s initialized from). Doesn’t that mean you’re wrong about latent reasoning architectures being less monitorable?</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.20560"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This paper</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> by GDM studies the monitorability of DiffusionGemma, and reports that DiffusionGemma is similarly monitorable to Gemma4 (see Section 4 of their paper). However, this measurement should not provide much of an update on how monitorable one ought to expect large-scale latent diffusion models to be. The monitorability measured in section 4 only lets the monitor examine the model’s output (i.e. they do not look at any of the internal steps). To the extent that a diffusion model is capable of doing interesting computation within the denoising steps, and to the extent that it actually has a propensity to want to hide things from the monitor (neither of which seem particularly true for DiffusionGemma), it seems fairly likely that this sort of monitoring wouldn’t work.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The same paper also studies whether the latent states of DiffusionGemma are interpretable. (See section 3, and also </span><a href="https://www.alignmentforum.org/posts/QBuJ3suRZxrrxSTtv/does-diffusiongemma-do-latent-reasoning"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">this blog post</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.) It finds that DiffusionGemma’s performance is mostly preserved if you project its latent states back down to tokens. In other words, although the architecture supports latent reasoning, the model has not learned to perform interesting latent reasoning. This might seem to contradict our claim that latent reasoning architectures will make oversight harder: maybe some ways of training latent reasoning architectures don’t even teach the model to reason with latents, and therefore don’t compromise monitorability. We address this concern in </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#Appendix_B__Do_all_latent_reasoning_architectures_threaten_monitorability_"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Appendix B</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Q: It seems better for alignment if models gain capabilities from pretraining rather than from RL, since RL seems to lead to reward seeking and Goodharting. But architectural changes (including a switch to latent reasoning architectures) may be an important part of how we can keep getting more capabilities from pre-training. Does this mean that avoiding latent reasoning architectures is harmful for alignment?</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A: </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We think avoiding latent reasoning architectures is actually beneficial for alignment for a variety of reasons:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">First, let’s consider the argument: “it’s better for alignment if models get capabilities from pretraining rather than from RL”. This is plausible, although far from certain. However, we don’t think this implies that latent reasoning architectures are good for alignment. In particular, we think that latent reasoning architectures actually will get more of their capabilities gains from RL than from pretraining. We think being able to reason in a more flexible medium that can explore into novel ontologies and develop new concepts is more valuable during RL than during pretraining. Also, parallelism in training is more important in pretraining where we do teacher forcing than in RL where we are already necessarily doing inference rollouts. So, it might be more efficient to first pretrain a model, and then add in a latent channel, and mostly develop the latent channel during RL.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Latent reasoning architectures are harmful for alignment because they make it harder to notice and fix alignment problems.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Latent reasoning architectures make it more likely that AI models will reason in non-human ways. We speculate that AI models which reason in more human ways, via being more attached to the pretraining prior, are more likely to act in human ways, and alignment techniques motivated by the </span><a href="https://alignment.anthropic.com/2026/psm/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Persona Selection Model</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> are more likely to apply for them.</span></li></ol><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Appendix A: More on the necessity argument in the existing CoT paradigm</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Here we gather some more objections and evidence on the question as to whether an interpretable CoT will keep being necessary for models to accomplish hard tasks.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One concern about this line of argument is that models may be able to deliberately use the CoT to reason in ways that aren’t understandable to humans. However, so far, it seems like models </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.09714"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">find it hard to reason in even simple ciphers</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and more generally </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.05246"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">struggle to evade monitors when CoT is necessary</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Consistent with this, the Astra system card maintains that “</span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">if a task requires reasoning to complete, the model doesn’t seem to be able to hide such reasoning</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">”. We expect the models would find this easier if they had sufficiently large no-CoT capabilities, but that the current relatively slow trend in no-CoT capabilities wouldn’t clearly enable robust steganographic reasoning soon. Furthermore, if models at some point do start reasoning steganographically to evade oversight, we might be able to detect this via techniques such as measuring behavioral changes under paraphrasing. This would give valuable evidence about models’ degree of misalignment, and might allow us to find ways to solve the cipher or reduce this behavior.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Another way that CoT could become less useful for understanding models is if CoT naturally becomes illegible due to scaled up RL training — i.e., RL teaches the model a new language which humans don’t understand, allowing the models to perform the necessary reasoning without enlightening monitors about its content. Indeed, recent CoTs have become somewhat less legible, with more compressed speech and sometimes using hard-to-interpret jargon. However, to us, they don’t seem to be on a trend towards total unreadability over the next few years.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefyb6tcsvw40n"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnyb6tcsvw40n">[15]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> And if jargon or euphemisms started posing a real problem for interpreting them, it’s plausible that targeted research interventions could find ways to either reduce this drift</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefiam99hiqzxp"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fniam99hiqzxp">[16]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> or to “translate” the models’ new terms.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefgju3ycu568h"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fngju3ycu568h">[17]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> To judge for yourself how legible current CoTs are, you can browse some snippets </span><a href="https://stolen-thoughts.com/#gallery"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">here</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> from recent models. (And compare </span><a href="https://openai.com/index/learning-to-reason-with-llms/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">here</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> with old snippets from o1.)</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What empirical evidence do we have about how the “necessity of CoT” argument is holding up? Let’s look at </span><a href="https://www-cdn.anthropic.com/0339e6a7c5c7b87f5c07798616dc32c215d14235/Claude%20Fable%205.1%20%26%20Claude%20Mythos%205.1%20System%20Card.pdf#page=132"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">evidence from Anthropic’s latest system card</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, where models are tasked with accomplishing a task while avoiding a monitor.</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/f40753d191fa97cd748a4d73573f568bdf2281c05415dde832a9c6b872bbc7c6/jvt5oq2abcaskdpq1e0m" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Looking at this graph, we can observe that:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The hidden scratchpad condition is the most favorable for all models, suggesting that ability to monitor CoT is adding value.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">These experiments are clearly not only testing the necessity argument. Being given more affordances (more thinking) often harms models’ performance, implying that their propensities are harming their performance.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefkcptppqu97m"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnkcptppqu97m">[18]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There seems to be a rough trend for models to get less monitorable as they get more capable.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefejzsn0lv4z"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnejzsn0lv4z">[19]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> This seems adequately explained by more capable models having (i) greater ability to accomplish the task with less reasoning, and (ii) being better at controlling their CoT so that it doesn’t actively harm their performance. In particular, none of the models benefit from doing more reasoning in the reasoning field itself when it is monitored.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref386bskry505"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn386bskry505">[20]</a></sup></span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This broadly supports our picture that the necessity argument will become weaker for relatively easier tasks, as models’ no-CoT capabilities become sufficient to do them, but is likely to remain strong for tasks where the CoT is necessary.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">How reassuring is this? At the moment, it seems reasonably reassuring, since models would likely need more serial cognition than they can fit in one forward pass to accomplish tasks like “</span><a href="https://blog.redwoodresearch.org/p/ai-catastrophes-and-rogue-deployments"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">start a rogue internal deployment</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> while evading safeguards”, “reliably strategically perform well in training in order to gain power later”, or “plot to disempower humanity”. Whether it will stay infeasible for models’ no-CoT capabilities depends on the quality of control measures implemented at AI companies. If AI control measures were improved, then the amount of serial cognition required seems greater than what you’d get from multiple years of no-CoT capability improvement along the historical trend:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2606.07157"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Think Fast</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> calculates a doubling per year, and the historical trend from when that paper was written suggests that no-CoT time horizon would be around half an hour in 3 years. The tasks listed above all seem likely to take more than half an hour of serial reasoning given good control measures.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">However, Astra </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/eRmzz8J8Qkzqvzrgg/astra-can-do-a-concerning-amount-with-no-chain-of-thought"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">was a big jump in no-CoT capabilities</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> that wasn’t predicted by this trend. If we treat Astra as a one-time jump then we still get less than a day's worth of thinking per forward pass, which also seems like less than you'd need to do the above tasks given good control measures. If Astra-sized improvements keep on happening, however, the situation seems more concerning.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It’s worth noting that Astra’s increase in no-CoT capabilities might be downstream of an architecture change, and may therefore be an example of how architecture changes reduce the gap between no-CoT capabilities and with-CoT capabilities dangerously fast. We’re still missing enough public evidence to adjudicate this point.</span></li></ul><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Appendix B: Do all latent reasoning architectures threaten monitorability?</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There may be some ways to train latent reasoning architectures that wouldn’t compromise monitorability. In this appendix, we explain how this interacts with the claims in our post.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Models built on latent reasoning architectures won’t necessarily learn to use the latent channel for doing reasoning. For instance, suppose you took a standard transformer and modified it to pass latents in parallel with CoT tokens, but you didn’t train the transformer to use the latents well. Then, it might have similar monitorability properties to a standard transformer.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The claim we’re making in this post is that if latent reasoning architectures end up giving substantial capabilities gains, then this is likely because the model has learned to do some general purpose reasoning in latents, and we think this will make the model harder to oversee.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There might be some exceptions to this.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For example, it’s possible that the latents of a diffusion model like DiffusionGemma could make it a lot easier to train the model in the first place, even if </span><a href="https://www.alignmentforum.org/posts/QBuJ3suRZxrrxSTtv/does-diffusiongemma-do-latent-reasoning"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">they're basically equivalent to discrete CoT during inference</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As another example, it’s </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">conceivable</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> that the latents of something like a “full-bandwidth transformer” model could play a fundamentally supportive role to the CoT, without any ability to support general-purpose reasoning separate from the CoT. For example, if the latents were only ever trained during RL where the only source of randomness came from sampling tokens, there’s some argument that the model would have to keep the tokens in a central causal role in its reasoning, since that’s the only source of entropy that powers the RL.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefctox3npb0ks"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnctox3npb0ks">[21]</a></sup></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If the latents play a supportive role rather than supporting general purpose reasoning, it’s still possible that they would undermine monitoring. For example, it seems more likely that a model would use tokens in non-human-understandable ways if it can augment its representation of them with latents that capture non-human-readable nuances.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Nevertheless, it’s conceivable that the CoT would stay similarly informative about the model’s cognition.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So given this, why are we still arguing that latent reasoning architectures are dangerous, rather than arguing about a more narrow class of architectures?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One reason for this is that, in practice, it seems very hard to establish to what degree such a latent reasoning architecture and training process would undermine the value we get from the CoT. As a result, if AI developers made reasonably conservative choices about which latent reasoning architectures seemed safe to deploy, it seems unlikely that they would be able to get a lot of additional performance relative to normal CoT models.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For a full-bandwidth transformer, we would think it’s quite reasonable to conclude that the architecture can’t be used to circumvent the CoT if a serious elicitation effort, including IID training, was made towards this goal, and that failed. (And this was reviewed or done by third parties.) Indeed, this would directly address the concern that the architecture change caused a reduced gap between the model’s no-CoT capabilities and with-CoT capabilities. But we think that’s relatively unlikely for performant versions of these architectures.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Some AI developers may want to argue that IID training is an unnecessarily conservative method of elicitation, and that the whole reason to expect the architectures to </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">not</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> undermine CoT monitorability is that they </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">weren’t</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> ever trained to that end. But absent a conservative method of elicitation like that, it seems very difficult to provide strong reasons that the latent channel can’t be used to undermine the CoT.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In particular, AI developers will generally be motivated to tinker with different training procedures and implement the changes that lead to higher performance. This will create a constant pressure towards modifications that teach the model to use its latents for additional types of reasoning, </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">even if</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> the AI developers themselves don’t understand why the modification would generalize that way. It would be difficult to have targeted evaluations for whether the model learned to reason using the latent space, when we may not understand the mechanisms by which this happened. In addition, the most concerning types of misaligned AI would be actively motivated to sandbag on insufficiently elicited evaluations.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Another reason why we’re writing about the dangers of latent reasoning architectures is that we think coordinating to avoid such architectures seems particularly tractable, due to the relative ease of agreeing whether something counts as a latent reasoning architecture or not.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The main alternate coordination target we’re aware of is coordinating to maintain high monitorability. We think this is worse for several reasons:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It seems hard to abandon R&amp;D effort on an architecture paradigm that you’ve invested into, so avoiding these architectures altogether seems more promising than trying to stop doing these types of architectures after they have progressed to the point of being unmonitorable.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The problem of evaluating whether models are monitorable is quite challenging and requires lots of scientific judgement. This makes it unfortunate as a coordination target because there is lots of room for disagreement and motivated reasoning when arguing about monitorability.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We’re worried that if people pursue latent reasoning architectures, there might be fairly straightforward ways to modify these models to use their latents for reasoning. Coordination seems much trickier if it is very easy to defect.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Today’s monitorability evaluations simply don’t seem strong enough to screen off the evidence from architectures.</span></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Overall, while it’s possible that some latent reasoning architectures could preserve our ability to understand models’ reasoning and cognition, we think that AI developers should have a strong default presumption to avoid them, and have a high evidential bar for overcoming this presumption.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Appendix C: Comparing specific interpretability techniques with CoT</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The most important reason for why we think the CoT is more valuable than other types of interpretability is by direct comparisons to the state of the art in interpretability. Here are some examples of techniques and their limitations compared to the CoT:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/research/probes-catch-sleeper-agents"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Probes</span></b></a><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> CoT gives a fairly “holistic” picture of the model’s reasoning. Many types of interpretability would lack this property even if otherwise they worked extremely well. For instance, probes are quite brittle: you get a single bit of information about the model’s thoughts, and that bit must be something you can effectively gesture at with training examples. Reliable probes could of course be extremely useful, but this low-bitrate issue means that they still won’t enable the same kinds of deep investigation as CoT.</span></li><li value="2"><a href="https://openai.com/index/how-confessions-can-keep-language-models-honest/"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Confessions</span></b></a><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Asking a model to give a post-hoc description of what it did and what it was thinking might be useful in many situations. However this method has important limitations:</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Trained confessions might learn to </span><a href="https://docs.google.com/document/d/1WwsnJQstPq91_Yh-Ch2XRL8H_EpsnjrC1dwZXR37PC8/edit"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">imitate human judgements</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> rather than truthfully report whether the model has done something wrong. In general, confessions won’t tell us anything that a human wouldn’t be able to verify for themselves, unless we get lucky with generalization.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A misaligned AI model could selectively omit information from its confessions.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">People will likely use confessions to notice bad behavior, and then try to remove this bad behavior—this puts selection pressure against confessions being honest.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AI models might not have great introspective access to their own reasoning. If you ask an AI model why it did something, it might rationalize a response. (For example, in follow-up experiments to recent cybersecurity incidents, Mythos 5 </span><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/research/alignment-assessment-cybersecurity-incidents"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">does not consistently acknowledge</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> that its actions could cause harm when asked follow-up questions.) Alternatively, the AI might guess what a human carefully inspecting its actions would think its confession should be (a central </span><a href="https://docs.google.com/document/d/1WwsnJQstPq91_Yh-Ch2XRL8H_EpsnjrC1dwZXR37PC8/edit?tab=t.0#heading=h.kkaua0hwmp1d"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">ELK</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> concern), rather than giving the true answer.</span></li></ul></li><li value="3"><a href="https://transformer-circuits.pub/2026/nla/"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">NLAs</span></b></a><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">NLAs are copies of the model that are initialized based on guesses about what the model’s reasoning might be. This raises concerns about collusion and about hallucinations, and about the NLA output not really being representative of the target model’s thoughts. There aren’t especially strong theoretical reasons to think that the NLA output is telling you important properties (such as misalignment-relevant properties) of the model’s thoughts: they can probably achieve good reconstruction loss without this (and the NLA’s outputs are </span><a href="https://turntrout.com/natural-language-autoencoder-robustness"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">sensitive to the initialization</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">). Especially if the warm-start trained the model to output benign thoughts when the AI was actually having malicious thoughts, you might expect it to never learn to verbalize malice. Furthermore, the reconstruction objective of NLAs in principle incentivizes “input inversion” (verbalizing the context verbatim) and steganography (to encode more information about the hidden states than you could with standard English), which though currently not observed could emerge with more optimization.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Another concern is that a switch to latent reasoning architectures might harm existing interpretability techniques. For example:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The </span><a href="https://transformer-circuits.pub/2026/workspace/index.html#intro-jlens"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Jacobian Lens</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> works by measuring how representations increase the probability that models will output certain tokens, and would likely get weaker if models converted a smaller fraction of their thoughts to tokens.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Researchers often interpret the representations found in </span><a href="https://transformer-circuits.pub/2024/scaling-monosemanticity/" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">SAEs</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> by seeing how they affect token outputs and are affected by token inputs, which may also get less reliable if models use tokens for much less of their cognition.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">NLA initializations depend on a language model speculating about models’ internal states based on its token inputs and outputs.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">More generally, if models don’t represent CoT in natural language, this might make their internal states less likely to cleanly map on to human concepts, which might make all interpretability techniques work less well.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefxrylybyko6i"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnxrylybyko6i">[22]</a></sup></span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fn2rzgz2x5opx"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref2rzgz2x5opx">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If a model is trained to reason using continuous vectors (rather than just language) it would be natural to let different copies of that model send each other messages in the form of sequences of such vectors. They could also store memories of this form for extended periods of time.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnlkinkaiyj7o"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreflkinkaiyj7o">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In the standard CoT paradigm, a single forward pass uses a fixed number of transformer layers in series (somewhere around 100 layers at most in open source models), after which the model must select a token to output. This token is the only information that gets passed to the beginning of the next forward pass. (Later forward passes can also, at each layer, attend to information from previous forward passes that happened at the just-previous layer. But this doesn’t increase serial depth.)</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fngl4n8myu95j"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefgl4n8myu95j">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This could be explained by how increased no-CoT capabilities from typical pretraining improvements </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">also</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> improve with-CoT capabilities, but some improvements (especially RL) will disproportionately improve with-CoT capabilities. A switch to latent reasoning architectures could result in a massive disproportionate increase in no-CoT capabilities relative to with-CoT capabilities, compared to the historical relationship between these.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As an exception to the trend, the difference between no-CoT capabilities and with-CoT capabilities may have shrunk for Astra. We don’t know whether this was due to an architectural change or not. (OpenAI employees have claimed that it wasn’t, but </span><a href="https://www.theinformation.com/articles/secret-technique-behind-openais-astra-model-sparks-security-concerns"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">leaks</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> indicate that Astra does use a significantly different architecture, which seems like one of the most likely explanations for a change like this, so we want to see more evidence.) Regardless, the difference between Astra’s with-CoT and no-CoT capabilities remains enormous, and it doesn’t seem like Astra’s shift is part of a consistent trend.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">See also the FAQs for an argument that no-CoT capabilities will stay serially constrained in the current paradigm, by default.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnsf2ivpgcuwg"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefsf2ivpgcuwg">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This raises questions about why they’d be adding the latents in the first place. But there are some conceivable ways that latents could augment the CoT reasoning without supporting general-purpose reasoning on their own.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fngza9365fdmm"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefgza9365fdmm">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">CoT controllability is measured by prompting models to write CoTs in particular ways, and seeing if they’re able to complete the instruction. If models can complete it, that indicates that they have flexible “control” over their CoT, and could likely also modify it to avoid monitoring if that was instrumentally useful for their goals. Importantly, models have probably not been intentionally trained to make their CoT conform to particular content instructions, since OpenAI describes CoT controllability as an undesirable property, and generally assert that they don’t train on the CoT. So their ability to control the CoT in response to prompts suggests that they may have generalized into a fairly flexible ability to control it. Once models have high CoT controllability, it seems like any argument for why they would have a high propensity to articulate thoughts in their CoT would have to rely on an </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">alignment</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> argument that their goals are consistent with such articulation — but such alignment arguments seem hard to make and we’d very much prefer a world where CoT monitoring arguments could provide a separate check.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnf7mxgxikme"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreff7mxgxikme">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The case for generalization is especially plausible when it comes to CoT </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">controllability</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> — i.e., models seem likely to generalize from being trained to flexibly control their output tokens to being able to flexibly control their CoT tokens. And indeed, the trend is heading in this direction, as discussed in the previous section.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnyqc411spxjm"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefyqc411spxjm">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Though this is relatively lower in OpenAI models, which suggests that it might be due to particular choices about training, and that it could be mitigated.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnkl2bfwdrqh"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefkl2bfwdrqh">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One might have hoped that the auxiliary CoT might play the role of an accurate summary of the accompanying latent reasoning, especially as it may be more causally relevant to outputs than e.g. post hoc confessions. However, it seems inefficient to use the CoT to repeat large amounts of latent reasoning rather than do additional computation (or at least only produce very compact summaries of such reasoning), so CoTs that faithfully represent latent reasoning are still less incentivized.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnhsnevkv3tdr"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefhsnevkv3tdr">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Confessions are a bit of a non-central example of an interpretability method. We count it since a key difference between normal monitors and confessions is that the confessing model can use the activations of the model it is evaluating.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnodsvcxmdwrh"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefodsvcxmdwrh">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Why is this? During training, RL samples from a probability distribution that was originally based on the human prior, and has only been gradually adjusted away from it.
